ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-04.ana.ud528e187	This issue concerns the introduction of national legislation for the erasure and storage of traffic data under strict conditions which also meet the minimum requirements of the EU Data Storage Directive. The proposition was delivered to Stortinget on 10 December 2010. The majority, the Right Party and the Working Party reinforce the data protection requirements of the Code on the storage, storage and use of traffic data. In addition, a number of key privacy principles in this and other areas of society are significantly strengthened and improved. The majority point of departure has been to ensure a national law that balances data protection with anti-crime considerations – not just to decide on the Data Storage Directive. At the same time, legislative changes are an important contribution to the protection of privacy. In a very short time, it has become so that technology permeates our everyday lives. Privacy is under pressure in many areas of society. In order to safeguard privacy, strict rules for the collection, storage and deletion of data are introduced. Examples include a six-month retention period, a police record in the provision of data, a high level of penalties and an obligation to delete, and the strengthening of control activities by the Data Protection Supervisor with the echo operators. As a result of the agreement presented today, the legal security, privacy and security of individuals are strengthened. The new principle of the implementation of the Data Storage Directive is that providers are required to store and that storage is to be carried out for a purpose other than for the provider’s own, namely the fight against crime. Traffic data is being stored today. Traffic data is currently being used by the police to combat crime. With the new regulations now in force, we will store a few more data than today, and the storage time will be slightly longer. At the same time, the use of these data is being put under significantly stricter controls and conditions than are currently in force. The Committee has conducted a comprehensive, open and wide-ranging consultation. Discussions have been conducted with all the parties in this case – this to further discuss the process, the parties’ balances and important points of view in the case, and to try to reconcile key points. In all discussions, the parties were made aware of the overall right-wing approach to national legislation and the strengthening of privacy. Many participants in the debate on the Data Storage Directive have argued that today’s legal situation is good and unproblematic. That's wrong. More than ten years ago — long before the EU adopted its directive — Økokrim and Kripos pointed out that their access to traffic data from telecommunications and data traffic was uneven and depending on different procedures by different eco-suppliers. Postal and telecommunications regulators have long been skeptical of the current state of law, because the practices of telecommunications operators with regard to extradition vary. Data is often stored longer than required by the legislation, the deletion obligation is not respected and location data is stored in violation of current legislation. Since 2000, four public investigations have suggested that today’s legislation is inconclusive and poor. The purpose of the EU Data Storage Directive is to harmonise the legislation on the storage of defined data generated by the use of electronic communications. The aim is to provide police and judicial authorities with a tool to detect, investigate and not least prosecute serious crime, even across borders. Providers of public electronic communications service or networks, so-called eco-suppliers, currently store data for their own communication and billing purposes in Norway. The systems for storing traffic data are different, the extent of what is stored is different and the safety of this information is uneven. Traffic data is crucial in the investigation of serious crime. Telephone services are used by police and prosecution authorities, inter alia: in order to identify contact patterns, and mainly in cases involving more involved, such as drug trafficking, serious crime, trafficking in human beings and other organised crime. An example of this is ‘Operation Broken Lorry’, where a Moroccan drug network was rolled up by Kripos in 2006. A total of 35 were sentenced throughout Europe, and two of the backers were sentenced in Norway to 21 years in prison. Location data is considered to be highly privacy-invasive due to the fact that much excess information about innocent people is generated by base station searches. The majority position of the Committee addresses this by requiring higher penalties to collect these base station data, and it is higher than for other data – five years. Internet traffic is particularly relevant in cases where offences are committed in whole or in part on the network. A current example is the dissemination of child abuse images. The storage of information relating to IP addresses to a particular subscriber is essential for initiating investigations into these cases at all. We have seen several examples of serious issues that could not have been investigated because these data have been deleted. Opinions from the PST Police Security Service (PST) at the hearings of the Committee are about Norway becoming more terrorised without the Data Storage Directive and that the PST uses traffic data in all its cases. In its hearing to the Ministry of Transport, the Advocate General pointed out that: ‘[in]the implementation of the Data Storage Directive, the police and the prosecution authority lose a central and important instrument in investigating and rectifying serious crime. It will provide broad legal certainty for citizens who reasonably expect law enforcement authorities to do their tasks at least as well tomorrow as today. Instead, a system is established whereby criminals can communicate freely without any danger of the authorities being able to trace the contact in retrospect, which is something new in our fight against crime.’ [[even a short time after]] Preliminary data from the ongoing EU evaluation demonstrate that the police are most in demand of data younger than six months old, although some data are also requested until they are 12 months old. The police are currently seizing the data stored, regardless of whether they are stored as a result of a breach of the erasure obligation. This means that if we say that today's legal status should be preserved, while following up with stricter supervision and disclosure requirements so that data is not illegally stored, the police's ability to solve or prevent serious crime or terrorism will be worse than it is today. The agreement between the Right Party and the Working Party will strengthen the obligation to delete traffic data at the end of the storage period. Providers shall be responsible for the storage themselves and a number of requirements are introduced to strengthen safety by storing them. Individual safety, legal certainty and privacy are ensured by the introduction of new and more stringent rules for the processing of traffic data: there must be a concession and there are penalties linked to its breach. Authorisation is required for personnel handling this type of data. Closed storage is introduced. Provisions shall be made for encryption. The retention period shall be six months and the supply of traffic data shall be subject to judicial decision. Today, there is no requirement for the severity of the criminal offence. Therefore, with this amendment, the threshold for collecting traffic data is significantly higher. Penalties are imposed, usually four years, and five years upon withdrawal from the base station. The deletion obligation is made clear. Commodity providers shall take responsibility for the storage themselves. I refer to what I have mentioned in relation to concession. Currently, stored traffic data are widely used by the police, but without legal requirements. Now it's being introduced. From now on, there will also be a new data protection regime in this part of the process. In addition, we also need to ensure that the urgency is not used unnecessarily by one court to have a watch regime to ensure continuity and availability of justice. Data supervision shall be significantly strengthened. I would like to stress that the protection of journalists and other editors from surveillance needs to be strengthened. In addition, the obligation of professional secrecy for all employees of law firms is strengthened. The provision of data in emergency situations is currently not regulated. Here the parties agree that we should take a closer look at the design of a legal provision in this area. The new principle is also to facilitate the logging of internal calls into personal registers with sensitive data in Nav and a number of processing registers in the healthcare system. It is a paradigm shift that the principle of the obligation of logging and the right of access to public registers should now apply. I recommend the Committee's position on the decision in this matter.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-04.ana.ud528e299	First of all, I would like to thank the Committee on Foreign Affairs and Defence for the flexibility it has shown in this matter. Not only were we asked to speed up the release by one week, but also to merge two debates – which I, as the case-rapporteur, initially had no desire to do. I saw it as unnatural to debate the implementation of a directive if it was not a majority of the directive, so my wish was that we had taken the debate on consent at a later stage. I also think I have to say something about the process in the matter. On 28 March we were informed – on the news – that the Workers' Party and the Right had reached an agreement, while the case rapporteur had not been informed about this. It thus made her work impossible before she got a draft agreement. On 29 March I received a draft agreement from the Transport and Communications Committee when they took their case temporarily, and the Foreign and Defence Committee received its opinion. We will deal with it on March 30, at 2.30 p.m. 13.00, on which the Committee on Transport and Communications gave its opinion at 1 p.m. 2:00 p.m. We in the Foreign Affairs and Defence Committee had our first review of the case for consent at noon. 13:00 Wednesday 30 March, and then to give our call Thursday 31 March at Nine o'clock. This shows that the matter to those degrees has been under time pressure, and that therefore, it is also right to ask questions about Storting's treatment in the case. Then I've been told what I mean about the process, and I'm going over to Prop. 50 S for 2010-2011 on consent to participation in a decision of the EEA Committee on inclusion in the EEA Agreement of Directive 2006/24/EC on the storage of data generated by the use of public electronic communications service or public electronic communications networks, also referred to as the Data Storage Directive. Everything started with the terrorist attack in Madrid in March 2004. The EU adopted a declaration on combating terrorism following it. The declaration saw the need for instruments to combat terrorism, and Sweden, the United Kingdom, France and Ireland presented a proposal for a Council decision on data storage. The Council proposal was rejected by the European Parliament in 2005, so it was rejected. The Commission then presented a proposal for a Directive, which was adopted by the Council and Parliament in March 2006. The Directive leaves to the national authorities to address several important issues, including: questions of access to and disclosure of data stored. The storage period may be between 6 and 24 months and national authorities may designate some to supervise the storage and security of data. The deadline for the implementation of the Data Storage Directive was September 2007 but with the possibility of deferral until March 2009. Article 14 of the Directive required the EU Commission to present an evaluation of the implementation of the Directive and its impact on economic activities by 15 September 2010, and not least on consumers. This evaluation is delayed and the date of submission of the evaluation is constantly being shifted. Since the implementation of the Data Storage Directive requires changes in the law, the Storting must give its consent under the second paragraph of Section 26 of the Constitution to make this Directive binding. So here we are today to make the directive binding, and there are many of us who do not want it at all. I do not go into the legislative changes and substance of the matter, such as the relationship with human rights, the police's need for data to combat crime, privacy and competition in electronic communications. It will be discussed by representatives of the Transport and Communications Committee, but I look at what has happened in Europe, what can be the consequences of an evaluation in the EU, and ask: Why do we have so much time? Several EU Member States have still not implemented the Data Storage Directive in their legislation — some because of constitutional objections to its implementation, others because of its economic nature and others because of the lack of storage capacity. Sweden, which helped to promote the proposal in 2004, has decided a few weeks ago to wait a year for implementation. Ireland, which was also one of the proposers, has filed an action against the Directive because they believe that the Directive is wrong in relation to the EC Treaty. If so, it is not a directive that is binding on the EEA countries either. In addition, we know that the EU is currently carrying out an evaluation of the Directive, albeit overtime. The rapporteur had planned a trip to Brussels to talk to the chairperson of the Evaluation Committee to hear on which points there could be significant changes in the evaluation. We were offered to meet her advisers, but unfortunately we never got it because of the speed of delivery. So we're waiting in suspense for what's coming. It's hardly any surprise that the committee is divided into the position in the case, and I assume that the majority, the Labour Party and the Right, will explain the position, and then I will certainly, using the rest of the privacy alliance, account for the minority proposal. The minority does not agree to participate in a decision of the EEA Committee on the inclusion of the Data Storage Directive. The main reason is of course that we believe this violates privacy. Personally, I also believe that it violates the Constitution § 102 and the European Convention on Human Rights Article 8. There is reason to believe that the evaluation concludes immediately and that substantial changes will be made to the Directive in the light of this. Therefore, the minority is willing to use the right of reservation in this case, which is the only way to avoid the Directive. It has been said very impeccably from the supporters of the directive on the consequences of using the right of reservation in this case – from almost total failure to EEA cooperation to exclusion from important forums in Europe. But this is nothing but speculation. We have never used the right of reservation before, and therefore we do not know the consequences. In any case, we can decorate ourselves with being second best in all of Europe to implement directives. It's just Malta that's better than us. That's what it says. In many other international forums, countries continue to veto, which is also the right of reservation, without significant impact on the relationship between countries. In the case of such a controversial case as the Data Storage Directive is in Norway, both the Labour Party and not least the Right should have taken the trouble to listen to people. People don't want any further surveillance, and they don't want to jump either because the EU says we should. Very few of us had liked that an agent had followed us around and noted where we were, who we talked to, and when we talked to them. But as soon as this becomes technological and invisible, it's not dangerous. Well, the countries that actually had physical surveillance are the countries that today do not want the Data Storage Directive. It should give us a picture of what we are going to adopt today, and it should also give not least Right something to think about. I would like to read the following: ‘Personal protection must be ensured in all areas of society, management, employment, business and criminal justice. When assessing the need for any new investigation methods, privacy considerations must weigh heavily. With technological innovations, both the transparency of personal circumstances and the ability to monitor persons has been greatly expanded, both publicly and privately. The extended scope of old and new forms of registration may represent a threat to privacy and freedom of expression.» So this quote is from the Right Principle Program, and I guess I don't have to say anything anymore. Unfortunately, we do not get a majority to say no to the Data Storage Directive. That is why, in the light of the process of processing, the economic consequences and since we know that an evaluation by the European Commission is soon to be carried out, the minority wants to promote a postponement proposal that we have submitted in time, which is likely to be distributed a little later, and which we want to vote for when our proposal for no consent falls. There are several reasons for postponement. One is, of course, the evaluation, as I have mentioned. The second is the current case brought by Ireland against the Directive, because Ireland is in favour of the case, yes, it is hardly necessary for Norway to deal with it at all. But at the moment we adopt consent, it is binding. I see that the time runs out, and I will therefore address the proposals made by the minority in this matter.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-04.ana.ud528e404	The cord shift is over.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-04.ana.ud528e408	Technological development creates opportunities. In a short time, mobile phones, iPads and laptops have become necessary tools for all of us in everyday life. Technology is central to the value creation of business and it is essential for the functioning of government. On average, each of us spends almost an hour and a half a day on the Internet, according to media outlets. Technological development is also a challenge. The technology is basic international. It may not be regulated solely by national law. That's where a lot of the disagreement lies. Technology is often central when criminal acts are planned and committed, and thus when crime is to be combated. Technology also puts privacy under pressure. A large number of personal data can be recorded, stored and systematised and others’ access to my personal data simplified. We leave a lot of electronic tracks behind, and commercial actors use our personal data in a commercial context. As politicians, we have a great responsibility to meet these challenges. We are responsible for providing police and prosecution authorities with tools to ensure that they can carry out the social mission that Stortinget has given them – to expose and combat crime. We are responsible for the protection of privacy and for ensuring that sensitive information is not compromised. Combining these two considerations is a demanding balance. I, and the Workers' Party, believe that the bill and the agreement that has now been concluded between the Workers' Party and the Right on changes in the Ecom Act, control this balance. On the one hand, we ensure police access to an important tool. There is no doubt that traffic data has been important in clearing up killings, trafficking in human beings and organised crime, such as the Novas. On the other hand, we are now introducing strong data protection in relation to today's situation, where there are now few or no rules around, among other things. access to the stored data. It has been important for us to listen closely to the recordings that came both through the consultation and consultation in the committee, and I would like to thank the Right for a good and constructive cooperation, which today results in a national law that strengthens both the fight against crime and privacy. Ekoom providers have long stored communication data for commercial purposes. However, the experience with very different practices and changes in the storage needs of providers means that this is no longer sufficient. We rely on communication data to be stored for community reasons. For this reason, the Workers' Party and the Right will impose a storage obligation on the echo operators for data that says something about the means of communication that have been in contact, where the communication has taken place, when and how. We agree that the data should be stored for six months. The content of the communication shall not be stored. In the file, the opposition claims that this is monitoring. It's not. Monitoring involves an active and continuous follow-up to what some are doing. Our proposal has nothing to do with monitoring. Initially, the police do not have access to any data, and they will only have access to selected data under certain conditions. I've been to Interpol, Kripos and children's houses all around the country, all of which fight sexual abuse against children. Information technology makes it easy for adults to make contact with children. Therefore, the Stortinget agreed that it was necessary to adopt a ‘grooming’ paragraph prohibiting adults from attracting children to contact. Information technology also causes child abuse images to spread at a speed and to an extent that we have never experienced before. But in several cases, Kripos has had to stop the investigation because data has been deleted. In contrast to what the representative, Woldseth, said, has led to a worsening of the police's ability to solve this type of serious crime. The last example is Europol’s roll-up of the world’s largest pedophile network of more than 70,000 users. 34 Norwegian IP addresses are connected to the network, but cannot be tracked because data has been deleted. Police officer in Kripos, John Ståle Stamnes, told the evening post that storage would last for six months to track down people in this case. And let me be clear: in matters like this, there is no – none – information that can replace traffic data when the police are going to move on in the investigation. Therefore, I think it is extremely serious that the minority only adds up to a continuation of today's practice of storing IP addresses for three weeks. Not even the Data Protection Authority's own proposal for a five-month storage period of IP addresses is endorsed by the minority. The consequence of this will be that Norway will still not be able to participate in the detection of serious criminal offences such as this, or we will have to rely on assistance from other countries that store traffic data any longer. The Working Party and the Right ensure a significant intensification of privacy in relation to today. We impose a requirement that data should be provided only after judicial review, and we introduce a general rule that this can only be done in cases involving serious crime, which has a penalty framework of four years or more. In addition, provision of traffic data shall be required for the investigation. This is not the situation today. Today, there are few or no conditions attached to the delivery of data to the police by the echo operators, and this tightening will lead to a narrowing of police access to data. The agreement also contributes to a tightening of the rules for securing the stored data, so as not to allow unauthorised access. We make it clear that there is an obligation to delete data, that there is a requirement for authorisation and closed storage by the echo operators, and that the Data Protection Authority is empowered to impose encryption of data that will fall under the storage obligation. We also assume that the Data Protection Authority significantly strengthens the control activities against the ecosystem providers and the justice sector. Following the presentation of the agreement last Monday, there has been a debate on the costs of increased security. And commercial actors can therefore use much more sensitive data for example. marketing aimed at each of us. As politicians, we cannot let the commercial needs go ahead of the need we as societies have to fight serious crime. If we do that, we'll fail both the police and privacy. The majority today ensure that we withdraw control of an important part of the fight against crime from market forces. In this way, we strengthen legal certainty for all of us.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-04.ana.ud528e507	The Data Storage Directive requires you to store an extensive amount of information about our mobile and network usage – who you call when you call, where you call, how long you talk, who you send mail to, who you receive when you are on the Internet, and how long you are there. SV is opposed to the introduction of the Data Storage Directive for three reasons. First of all, it's our strong over-interpretation that every single human being is innocent until the opposite is proven. We shouldn't be based on a think-tank in our state of law where we're ordering to gather tracks about each of the North's people, since we can get guilty. Then we pray to change the status of each human being, from innocent to potentially guilty. Secondly, the obligation to store large amounts of data increases the risk of harm to the environment and to abuse. Saving poses a risk to privacy. I don't really like the word ‘personal protection’ – it sounds like something we’re going to stop and sit in a museum. Integrity is a better word in my view. Who you are calling, to whom you are sending e-mails, is the information that may mislead you – for example: an e-mail from an additional member of the SV's group to the Labour Inspectorate on the relationship of the group, four phones to the crisis centre last weekend from a female representative, a secret tip to a journalist, repeated emails to an address ending with swingersnorge.com. On the contrary, such information may violate the integrity of the parties involved. Third, as long as you're innocent, there's no problem, some say. I mean it's the opposite. I mean, the problem is that we're innocent. Suspects can ensure the storage of information about already, and maybe this should be better. The serious thing now is how the directive can affect anyone who is not suspected of crime. In a 2008 study by the German Forsa Institute, more than half of them replied that they would not use telecommunications equipment when talking to psychologists, marriage counselors, or drug abuse as a result of the Data Storage Directive. 11 pst. replied that they had already refused to use telephone, mobile phone or e-mail in certain cases. We're not interested in innocent people holding back or letting's search out knowledge of fear of being shrugged over shoulders. Malte Spitz, parliamentarian for the Greens in Germany, went to court to get teledata on himself from Deutsche Telekom, and got it. Then he gave it to the newspaper Die Zeit, who along with posts from his blog and Twitter could construct the movement pattern of his day by day for half a year. On 1 September 2009, they have his movement pattern for 20 hours, on 4 September in 16 hours. Most days they have such an overview of him. Go online and check for yourself - interesting reading. The information that comes out can violate our integrity, but the knowledge that it is stored can in itself reduce our freedom. Although SV is strongly against the directive, I would say that we are even more concerned about the gradual change in attitudes, opportunities and policies that are taking place. We already have a comprehensive storage of data, and we have a comprehensive camera surveillance. Internationally, or here in the country, it is discussed to register online searches, to insert a piece into every car that can tell you where they are. We want better and more comprehensive health records that can connect, better registers with more information about children in kindergarten and school. We've been trying on space listening as well. I know several of these suggestions well. They're all well meant and well grounded in their field. But do we understand and think about the consequences of the sum? The French philosopher Jacques Attali, who was also a longtime adviser to President François Mitterrand, wrote the book «Short history of the future» on the theme. He describes a thoughtful future society in which the state monitors our health, diet, transport and schoolwork for the children – not because the politicians have been totalitarian, not for commercial reasons, but for functional reasons. The cost and public health are better with systematic recording of what we are doing. The school results are better when we know what the students are like and how they work. And everyone understands, of course, that the fight against crime is becoming more effective with the recording and storage of ever more data. Everybody's looking at it. We get more knowledge, society gets better and safer. The only thing we're losing is a little slack in social machinery. But I'm too slack. As long as it's sloppy, there's hope, because sloppy is a sign of freedom: the potato gold bag you shouldn't eat, the bypass you shouldn't take, the submission you cheated on – or more directly linked to the Data Storage Directive – the email you shouldn't have sent, the place you shouldn't have been one night. The freedom not to do it – or maybe even to do it sometimes – is invaluable to the individual. But it is also of invaluable value to society that we humans have a great freedom, an integrity, in which we can stand out. There must be not only room for the perfect life. There must also be room for smear in society. Of course, the Data Storage Directive does not lead to such a society, let me be clear about it. But the sum of a series of decisions and possibilities over time can lead us in such a direction. We record, monitor, store and use much more information now than for a decade or two. Furthermore, the road is important. What do we want to use this information for in 10 years? What data can we connect to to get more and better information? What will be the next time we register and save? In light of this, is it not wise to think again? It is said that one should let doubt come to the fore, and in this case my SV it would be reasonable: The EU's evaluation of the directive has not yet come. She will provide us with information on where EU legislation goes. Our neighbouring country Sweden has postponed the implementation. In several countries, the Directive is subject to legal proceedings after it has been known to be unconstitutional. Even if there's going to be a big country meeting in the future: when we have the opportunity to postpone it, and that's good reason, we should do it. I mean it has come a good thing out of the debate, which we currently end today. It's that I don't think it's the end. I think that's the beginning -- the beginning of a much more vibrant social debate about the balance between our freedom and integrity on the one hand and all the well-founded functional desire we have as a society on the other. We – the Biggest, the Government, the political system – must lead in a fundamental debate on this in the future. For a long time, we have seen individual social interests in the driver’s seat and in a fast-track sequence, as well as well-meaning and functional proposals for registration and monitoring in different fields. The principal questions we've been dealing with have been sidetracked. This is politics turned upside down. It must be the opposite – that politicians discuss and adopt the framework for social development, and then we ask for follow-up and discussion based on how the different aspects of society should be addressed. Whoever is willing to sacrifice his freedom for a temporary security, devours cork freedom or security, has said the American philosopher and scientist Benjamin Franklin. I want to add: in a freer society, we will feel safer than in a society where we have sacrificed much of our freedom. Let us believe that the wide-ranging debate on the Data Storage Directive means a clean-up of freedom and personal integrity as key issues in the societal debate.

The Data Storage Directive means that all residents of Norway are monitored in practice. Therefore, the Centre Party opposes the entry of the Data Storage Directive into Norwegian law. ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-04.ana.ud528e687	Great Brother, or “Big Brother”, is the alleged dictator in George Orwell’s novel “1984”. The book is about the fictitious totalitarian state of Oceania, which has developed the perfect mechanism for the control and repression of its citizens. It is written in the period between the experiences of World War II and the fear of the cold war. In the society described by Orwell, everyone is supervised by the authorities. ‘Storebrother’ is always present. “Big Brother” even enters the most intimate spheres of life. The population is constantly reminded of this by the slogan ‘Brother sees you’. A section from the book reads like this: ‘Of course, it was impossible to know whether one was being observed for a particular moment. One could just guess how often and according to which system the Thought Police tapped an individual line. It was even possible that it guarded everyone and everyone all the time. But at least it could tap your line anytime it wanted it. One had to live – one lived thanks to a habit that became an instinct – assuming that every sound a team was heard and every movement was examined except in the dark.” This was written in 1949. The Data Storage Directive – and let us stick to the facts – obliges all telecommunications companies to store who you call, where you call, when you call, how long you talk, who you text, where the receiver is, when you are on the Internet – how long – and who you send e-mail to, all for good reasons, in the service of good purpose, useful for the fight against crime. The Data Storage Directive means that in practice all residents of Norway are followed in all their movements in these media. You could call it monitoring or not, but this is a fact. That is why the Christian People's Party is against turning the Data Storage Directive into Norwegian law. The products resulting from this Directive are not proportionate. That is why the directive has recently been declared unconstitutional in the Czech Republic. The Romanian implementation was declared unconstitutional in 2009, and the implementation was reversed. The same thing happened in Germany in 2010. In Sweden, as we have heard earlier in the debate, we have seen that implementation has stopped. It is a great paradox that the Labour Party and the Right are so keen to implement a directive which, in its present form, will hardly ever be introduced in all EU countries. Why in the world is it going to be so urgent to have it implemented in Norway? The Christian People's Party is critical of schemes that rock the freedom of individuals to communicate freely without being monitored. The Data Storage Directive restricts privacy and freedom of expression. The Directive is based on a situation where suspects are monitored to see everyone as potential offenders. There's a big difference in principle from the way it is today. It's not a functional question, as the representative Solhjell quite correctly pointed out, it's a value question. The growth of the information society and the development of ever-new technology require that we have a clear and principled view on this issue: what is man worth, and what are the rights of individual citizens? How do we relate to an information flow that increasingly challenges the individual's right to privacy? A society is based on trust between authority and citizens. The instruments resulting from the Data Storage Directive are of such a nature that they contribute to weakening this trust between the State and the citizen, although I must say that the majority have made sincere efforts to remedy the damage caused to the package they have provided. The Data Protection Authority characterises the law as another step into the surveillance society. The majority in this room are rocking by the individual’s freedom to communicate freely without being monitored. Privacy and freedom of expression are threatened. A principle is introduced in which all are guilty until the opposite can be confirmed. In the fight against crime and terrorism, disproportionate measures are put in place, which, in the ultimate sense, contribute to a crisis of confidence between the state and the citizen. We know that privacy needs to be balanced against the need to combat crime effectively. In many cases, there will be good reasons to introduce stricter controls. Consideration must always be weighed. But if we do not have a conscious relationship with the fundamental principles we are considering, then we will almost go unnoticed by the basic principles of human value and the freedom of citizens, because arguments of value can always be put forward. Each step doesn't have to be dramatic, but it will be a dismantling of privacy based on the principle of ‘take it, then take it’.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-04.ana.ud528e839	The current legislative proposal on the implementation of the Data Storage Directive is about combating serious crime, but it is also about strengthening privacy in an area that, in the opinion of many people today, is under-protected, and it is about tightening up and getting a clear framework for the type of data to be stored, and not least what conditions should apply to get this out to the police when the police need it to fight and detect serious crime. I'm going to be the first to admit – something you might not get the impression once you've heard the debate so far – that these are two considerations that are difficult to balance. It's not just a stencil that can be put on, and then one has won and the other has lost. I don't suspect any parties in this room to have noble motives to do the best, both to fight crime and not least to take care of privacy. In this way, I have to say that for some of us, privacy is a sum of many relationships; we are talking about children, we are talking about victims of crime – it can be women, it can be adults of both sexes – who would very much promote their own privacy against being able to get a clarification, detection and prevention against the intrusion to which they have been subjected. You have to bring that with you. As I and the majority of the population see it, the bill as it now exists, in agreement between the Workers' Party and the Right, is an important and correct measure for the police to have the necessary tools to keep track of the development of crime resulting from the new technology, thus safeguarding the privacy of quite a number of vulnerable groups, as the Storing has said through several treatments to have an increased focus and increased protection. It is theoretical and unrealistic to believe that crime in today's society can be combated satisfactorily without this tool. I have to say I disagree very much with Trine Skei Grande's description that the police have virtually no use for this tool. In repeated, major cases, large fish have just been taken by means of traffic data. It's well listed in the proposition, it's media-focused issues, it's known methodology, and there's no criminal policy debate about these traffic data really played the role that the police claim they've done. I would like to illustrate the need for the police with a quote from the Police Directorate's hearing opinion on the matter: «With house burglars came, with money notes came false, with the train came train robberies, with the car came pro-milling and with the welfare state came social security fraud.» Crime follows societal developments, and the police must follow societal developments, including in terms of methodology. Society is through-digitalised, and the police must also have access to digital media in their efforts to combat crime. This is the strong driving force for the adoption of these rules as they are now being proposed, and – I kept saying – also as quickly as possible. The legislative proposal fully demonstrates the importance of traffic data in the fight against crime. Traffic data has been central to resolving as serious issues as killing, body damage resulting in death, serious drug cases, gross traffic, rape and sexual assault against children under 10 and 14 years of age. For example, traffic data has proved to be decisive in clarifying the case of the ‘Skrik’, the ‘Orderud’ case and the ‘Nokas’ case. Fortunately, to this day our country has been proud of extensive acts of terrorism. Historical traffic data are an important tool in the prevention and control of terrorism, and we need to ensure, for the future, that the responsible authorities have access to such necessary data. In this way, it is paradoxical that the Progress Party, which is now voting against these proposals, is in favour of its own proposal to lower the threshold for what is even more intrusive towards citizens in terms of combating terrorism, namely the need to be able to listen. In proposal 4, the Progress Party proposes to be able to listen, i.e. to lower the threshold, and to intensify the type of activity that I define as fully transparent monitoring. The fact that you have to listen to communication or listen to space must be far more intrusive than that telecommunications companies have an obligation to store when you have called, where you have called, and who you have called. Given this starting point, it has been important for me that data storage rules are designed to strengthen privacy as much as possible. I have to give credit to the Right for the suggestions that came from the Right meetings before going into the Storting with this matter, and the input that has come, but also for the negotiations and what has been made by these. It has contributed to the fact that the interests of privacy and the fight against crime have received double profits. The proposition shows that it is possible to find good and balanced solutions, and the agreement between the Right and the Workers' Party represents such a strengthening of privacy. Storage obligations relate to traffic data, location data, subscription and user data resulting from the use of telephone, Internet access, e-mail and broadband telephony. The content is not stored. In nature, therefore, the storage and delivery of data is a much more modest interference in privacy than, for example, the use of data. coercive agents such as listening or hiding camera surveillance. There we experience that the Progress Party chooses to go beyond what the majority does today. The storage time is proposed to be set at six months. With the exception of IP addresses, which are currently stored for up to three weeks, much of the data covered by the storage obligation is already stored for three or five months. The debate sometimes suggests that this is a regime change, or a kind of paradigm shift, against privacy. But de facto, we're facing a three- to five-month storage, which is happening today, and it's absolutely right, as most people have had, that there's an obligation to this. Most of the data requested today by the police are stored for less than six months. However, experience shows that data between six and twelve months of age are requested in the investigation of the most serious criminal offences, including: The killing cases. I am somewhat concerned about the actions that cannot be resolved in the future due to the fact that data should not be stored for more than six months. It is therefore important that the length of the storage period is one of the conditions to be evaluated after four years. In the extension of the purpose of storage to crime prevention, extradition shall be to the police and prosecution authorities in order to combat serious crime. Dispatching shall take place after the judgment of the court. These are the great promises of privacy made at Stortingen’s treatment today – the fact that there is now a clear barrier to the type of crime that allows the police to obtain this data, and not least the fact that it is subject to judicial control. For the provision of traffic data, the general rule is that there should be grounds for suspecting a criminal offence to be punishable by imprisonment for four years or more. For base station search, the requirement is five years – it is more inclusive. Communication between criminals is particularly relevant in organised crime. Therefore, if the offence is deemed to have been committed as part of organised crime, the penalty framework requirement has been reduced to three years. Finally, individual offenders are included in an exhaustive list of lower-criminal offenders, but where data are considered to be particularly central in the investigation. In addition to the fact that there must be grounds for suspecting a criminal offence covered by those conditions, data must be presumed to have a significant impact on the investigation. Many have expressed concern about the potential for ejaculation. I understand that. There is something that, no matter what the Storting should come to today, is going to worry us all. However, the question is now entirely in the hands of the Storting, in the sense that there is a need for legislative changes in order for data to be provided in other cases. It is an important principle of the legislative proposal that is now being adopted. One goes from a situation where, to a very large extent, it has not been permitted what kind of criminal acts provide the basis for the collection of data, to now establish that principle. It's a good protection against purpose slipping. The legislative proposal placed a great deal of emphasis on data security. This is further strengthened and clarified in the Agreement between the Workers' Party and the Right. And a number of requirements are introduced to strengthen the security of data storage, including: the obligation of concession and also that arising from the agreement in respect of encryption. In fact, we are in a situation where this bill continues to allow the police to use a very important methodology, while the majority of people contribute to the tightening up and clearing up of the data protection weaknesses that have been raised in the past. So I think this is a day that can help to strengthen privacy, especially for the most vulnerable, in order to give traditional privacy a strength, but not least to facilitate that the police who are faced with serious abuses against individuals and associations actually have the opportunity to investigate and find out who is behind it and to conduct an ordinary criminal procedure.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-04.ana.ud528e947	The cord shift is over.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-04.ana.ud528e950	The purpose of the Data Storage Directive is to harmonise the legislation on the storage of electronic traffic data in order to ensure that they are accessible to the police in the process of detecting serious crime. This directive was the subject of thorough debates in the EU bodies and in the Member States to strike a good balance between heavy-weight privacy considerations and the societal considerations behind it – the same debate that we have had. The fact that our debate comes several years after our neighbouring country took its decision underlines the limitation in our form of association with EU cooperation, namely that we do not participate in the joint European debate before the decisions are taken. It gives us a limited ability to influence, to say it cautiously. At the same time, through the EEA Agreement, we are obliged to participate in the harmonisation of the legislation provided for by this Directive. Those who refuse to include this Directive in the EEA Agreement point to the right of reservation. Article 102 of the EEA Agreement requires that there should also be common legislation in the EEA area to ensure legal certainty and uniformity in the internal market. Therefore, the agreement states that the parties shall endeavour to reach an agreement. The possibility of reservation arises indirectly from the requirement for agreement. This is an important point of the agreement, precisely because we do not participate in decision-making processes in the EU, and therefore cannot ensure that specific Norwegian matters or interests are taken into account, as the Member States can. This means that there has to be something special with or for Norway that makes the provisions of the EU have been adopted, not suitable for us. One such example is the Renewable Directive. Our renewable share is 60 pst. – three times the EU target. It is then obvious that the estimates for the increase each country is committed to are very poor for Norway. It is therefore necessary to negotiate an adaptation to Norwegian conditions, and Norway and the EU must therefore agree. Without such an agreement, no implementation. The Data Storage Directive is not of such a nature. The important differences between privacy and societal considerations on which this is based are no different in Norway than in our neighbouring European countries. We can't rely on any particular Norwegian circumstances that make this not true right here with us. As it has been adopted, it also provides a considerable scope for action so that we decide for ourselves key elements such as the length of storage, the safety of storage and the strictness of data collection requirements. The ever-recurring requirement of individual teams to use this reservation option does not address the policy content of the EEA Agreement. The whole point of the agreement is to harmonise rules in the internal market in order to ensure fair competition and legal certainty for businesses and people. The EEA Agreement does not provide for a free choice on the top shelf of the rules to be followed, but it has an emergency exit for situations where the common legislation does not capture quite specific Norwegian circumstances. To date, it has not been necessary to use this option, because we have come to understand the specific Norwegian needs justified by circumstances or traditions that differ from our European neighbouring countries, such as the wine monopoly regime and the use of differentiated employers’ fees in district policy. The Workers' Party does not agree to include the Data Storage Directive in the EEA Agreement because we have to. We say yes because we think that makes sense. It provides, as the law now stands, a much better regulation of the storage, deletion, storage and transparency requirements of traffic data than current legislation does, and will ensure that Norway can fully participate in the important EU cooperation on harmonisation, problem solving and coordination that has an impact on the competitiveness of the ecosystem sector. It is important for Norwegian companies. A little over two years ago, in February 2009, a unanimous decision was taken to implement the third EU directive on money laundering and to amend our legislation in line with the directive. What's this about? Well, it reinforces the requirements and legal provisions that require banks and other credit institutions to monitor the movements of all our accounts. Unlike traffic data storage, this is a real surveillance. The purpose is to detect illegalities, including: money laundering. The computer systems that follow any movement on our accounts alert us if anything suspicious happens. Many have experienced being called up by their bank and asked if they have control of the credit card because the alarm had gone – if so, for example. the card is charged in both Oslo and Madrid at the same time. People are pleased with this service because it reveals and prevents fraud attempts. But people get this help because their accounts are being monitored to see if we're going to do something illegal. This is what the so-called data protection alliance parties in today's settings refer to as: ‘to lay down the democratic rule of law that everyone is innocent until the opposite is proved to be true.’;» [[...]] This principleal limit of the legal state itself, which they claim we are trodden by the Data Storage Act, crossed once in the 1990s, when the supervision of our bank accounts started. If anything happens on our accounts that gives us grounds for suspecting that we are committing illegalities, not just the alarm will go. There is also a notification to Ecocrim, which will carry out more investigations and, where appropriate, carry out investigations. Between our accounts and Ecokrim, there is no court to decide whether Ecokrim should be made transparent. The law that all parties in this room agreed to in February 2009 prohibits the owner of the account from knowing that the alarm has passed and that he is in the searchlight of Økokrim. This has been going on since the beginning of the 1990s. The 2006 Annual Report of Ecocrim shows that over the past five years, Ecocrim has received 22,767 reports of suspicious transactions, but only a few end up in a conviction. We're all being monitored. Some come to Økokrim's searchlight, but very few have done something wrong. The Data Protection Authority was very sceptical about this intrusion that happened, and wrote in its hearing opinion that the police's extended arm is even longer. The balance between privacy and police needs was very central in the legislative proposal at that time. But unlike today, all parties say that the balance was acceptable, even from a privacy point of view. How could the parties that today say no, that time vote for laws that require banks to monitor the movements of our accounts, just to check if we were going to do something wrong? Where was the idea of the principles of the rule of law at that time, about two years ago? To store traffic data for six months, to require a court order to have access to them by the police on grounds of suspicion of serious crimes with a criminal record for many years is, according to these parties, to take a long step into the surveillance society, as they write in the suspension. But to oblige banks to continuously monitor the movements of our accounts, automatically send notice to Ecokrim and refuse to notify the account holder – and this is information that is stored for ten years – is not. So that's where you separate teams with us. For this is to monitor innocent people, without justified suspicion, such as, among other things. The representative, Højbräten, pointed out, as a matter of principle, unacceptable earlier in the debate. This is not a matter of intervention, and I hope some of these parties can explain to us during the debate how this can relate, if there is such a natural and logical context. The protection of privacy is important. But in some contexts it has to be weighed against heavy social considerations, as we have done in combating economic crime, and as we do today in relation to other serious crimes. But it is important that we constantly hold the live debate on the position of privacy in a society where technological development makes us all abandon electronic tracks that can be put together and abused in different ways. Therefore, I hope that all parties look forward to the big message that will come about the position of privacy and that will provide a good basis for this broad and important debate. Finally, it is also a bit funny that the greatest pecuniary concern of these new so-called data protection alliance parties about the law we are about to adopt today is that it should be too expensive to keep the data in a responsible and responsible manner. Concern about costs is usually not so prominent when these parties talk about their heart matters.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-04.ana.ud528e1242	Think of the following: You have an iPhone full of applications that continuously and automatically connect to the Internet 24 hours a day, to update on weather, sports, news, e-mail and other services. There are about 300,000 such phones in active use already in the country. If Norway introduces the Data Storage Directive, all these connections will certainly be registered and stored, plus information on where these connections were at the time. In other words, technological developments will lead to the effective monitoring of the data storage directive for at least six months and the retention of the data for at least six months. It's not a pleasant thought. iPhones were poor in Norway in 2006, when the Data Storage Directive was adopted in the EU. In a few years' time, technology alone could have made the Data Storage Directive quite unmanageable and the effects would be untransparent. These considerations are the journalist Frode Bjerkestrand in Bergen's Official Journal, and the Christian People's Party agrees with him in this. The Christian People's Party will stand up to arrangements that rock the freedom of individuals to communicate freely, without being monitored. The Data Storage Directive threatens privacy and freedom of expression. We may take our freedom and our rights for granted, but we must never forget the fundamental rights of every citizen. The current principle of the Data Storage Directive is that all traffic information from all citizens should be pre-stored for a certain period of time. As many have been involved in the debate in a reflective way, both those who are supporters of it and those who are opposed to it, it is that by definition, in principle, any attack on privacy is linked to a police intervention — that is, if the police are to do something, it is, by definition, an interference on privacy. On the other hand, of course, we have the situation on which our democracy is based, that it is the national assembly of the country that is just going to give the police authority the power to address personal issues in order to precisely solve crimes that are detrimental to society. It is the balance that this discussion is about. ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-04.ana.ud528e1710	For those of us who are concerned with both the fight against crime and privacy, it is good that we now have a majority on the Stortinget which ensures the implementation of the Data Storage Directive. For, as many have already been involved, a yes to the Data Storage Directive is not a choice between combating crime and privacy – that is, thank you both. The Data Storage Directive is about providing the police with the tools and methods they need to prevent and solve serious crime such as violence, rape and assault. This is crime that intervenes in the lives of individuals, which often brings great consequences to the victims. It is also about creating a framework for the transparency of stored data and for the use of these data. The implementation of the directive in Norwegian law is a regulation of access to the data that we know that telecommunications providers are already storing. It is a step forward for privacy, not a step backwards, as some have claimed. The Data Storage Directive responds to the evolution of crime in terms of change. This must be combated by modern technology, a technology that also presents new challenges, both in terms of police access to evidence and in terms of individual privacy. The criticism of the Data Storage Directive has been that the Directive weakens the rights of individuals. This I believe is to reverse the problem on my head. Because without data storage, the individual individual in some cases will be deprived of the opportunity to have his or her case resolved, because the police will not have access to critical information. This is a problem I want to challenge the opponents of the directive: what about the victims of serious crime? We are all aware of examples of a lack of data storage that has led to a breach not being resolved. I'll mention a famous example from March this year. Europol then revealed a child pornography network that identified 34 Norwegian IP addresses, i.e. Internet addresses, to 34 computers in Norway. As Kripos wanted to have access to the addresses, it turned out that they had already been deleted from Norwegian network providers because they are not allowed to store the addresses for more than three weeks. With knowledge of these addresses, the Norwegian police could have captured the physical address where there are people involved in the spread of child pornography and in the pedophile network. With the Data Storage Directive in place, the storage time for IP addresses would have been slightly longer, and these could have been revealed, and future abuses would have been prevented. The Party has stated that it is committed to finding a solution other than the EU Data Storage Directive. I will quote Hoksrud from the Nations on June 18th in 2010: ‘We want a solution that will ensure that the police do their job. But we don't want to give the police carte blanche." To the Hoksrud and the Advance Party, I would like to say the following: that's exactly what we're doing now. Our solution ensures that the police have done their job, while at the same time demanding when the police will have access to the data. We don't in any way give the police a blank authorization to extract stored data. On the other hand, we say that privacy is so important that in order for the police to have access to the data, there should be serious crime, and it is the court that has to decide whether the police will be given access to the specific case. These are limitations which mean that there is no reason to fear a so-called monitoring society, some of which are concerned about. When the Progress Party itself proposed data storage as early as 2005, it was without these criteria and restrictions that safeguard privacy. The Labour Party is committed to taking responsibility for ensuring police and prosecution authorities the tools they need to combat and investigate serious crime.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-04.ana.ud528e1730	I count myself a liberal person and am not a follower of any Tut-and-run justice policy. I was among those who, in 2009, applauded Minister of Justice Knut Storberget’s stop in so-called ‘preventive space and telephone interception’, the rules introduced by the Bondevik Government in 2005, which the Methodological Control Committee has later considered to be unconstitutional. Fighting crime is important, yes well, but privacy, the right to privacy, is a fundamental right. The effectiveness of an investigation method to combat crime is not enough. What society wants to achieve — the benefits — must be balanced against the intervention of those affected. All use and retention of personal information is interference with privacy. However, in a number of areas of society, we accept that personal information about us is kept and used because it serves other important purposes. The central thing is that the interference with privacy is not disproportionate to what you would otherwise like to achieve. There must be proportionality between intervention and purpose. The question is, if the storage of traffic data for six months contributes to the resolution and prosecution of serious crime, is this a proportionate intervention? My answer is yes. Many have been involved in the debate on the Data Storage Directive. That's good, because it contributes to increased awareness of privacy. Overall, there has been a good debate. Unfortunately, some have chosen a form that is primarily suitable for creating fear. The former Director of Data Protection Georg Apenes set the standard when he pulled in the GDR secret police station Stasi. In several readings, representatives of the Left and the Advance Party have used words as ‘police’. Today, the representative Trine Skei Grande has referred to the bulk of the population as ‘wicked’. Others have claimed that the rule of law and democracy are at stake. Of course it's not like that! The 20 EU countries that have implemented the Data Storage Directive, such as our neighbouring countries Denmark and Finland, have neither said goodbye to democracy nor the rule of law. Those who suggest something else are trying to scare us into believing that this is a fight between the rule of law and the police state, between democracy and dictatorship, between black and white. This rhetoric creates a climate of debate that no one really deserves. In addition, such opinions help both to legitimise the mistrust of the authorities and the police and to suspect that – data storage directive or not – privacy and crime prevention must be balanced against each other. The representative Sandberg used part of his statement to accuse the undersigned and other representatives of the lie. The background is that the Progress Party has called for the DNA testing of immigrants as an action against coercion and terrorism. In my reply earlier today, I was injured to say that the measure would include first- and second-generation immigrants, as I understood the proposal. If forced marriage is to be prevented, it will not be possible to test one of two parties. I now understand at Sandberg that this is not the way the Progress Party has planned this arrangement. I would therefore like to make it clear that I now understand that it is only the very first generation of immigrants Progress will test the DNA. It is equally fully a serious interference in privacy and a gruesome expression of a ‘us and them’ tanker. But the right one should be right. Norway’s main drug issue was clarified because, unlike Norway, the Netherlands had data storage rules. The Data Protection Authority’s own study concludes that traffic data are crucial in criminal matters. The same conclusion was reached recently by EU Commissioner Cecilia Malmström, the Swedish People's Party, on the basis of figures from 20 EU countries. Preventing and making it possible to prosecute terrorism, identity theft or exploitation of children on the Internet is not just about combating crime. It's also about privacy. Recently, the police received tips about a father who operated as a hallik for his seven-year-old daughter on the Internet. The father informed me that he himself misused the daughter, attached three pictures of her and provided an e-mail address. When the police got the tip, the man's IP address was deleted from the Internet service provider, in line with today's storage rules. The matter has not been resolved. On Wednesday 16 March, Europol announced that they had rolled up the world’s largest pedophile network. An estimated 230 abused children have been identified in 30 different countries. As Kripos wanted access to the Norwegian IP addresses that had been used, the data had been deleted. Such stories make impressions, and they place the minority’s comments in a strange light in which they go against the Data Storage Directive of ‘respect for children’. The minority refers to the opinions of the Children's Ombudsman and Red Barna. But Redd Barna's letter of hearing says: ‘Save Children considers that the State’s obligation to protect children from abuse is far-reaching and can in some cases be overlooked in the interests of privacy.’ Fear The Children ‘explains assessments of alternatives to the Data Storage Directive’. They go on to say, ‘This must be done before Redd Barna finally takes a decision on the implementation of the Directive in Norway.’ The Children’s Ombudsman concludes that they do not find any basis for deciding on the Data Storage Directive. Without having concluded, therefore, the child-care consultancies are calling for alternatives to the Data Storage Directive. The Progress Party, SV, the Center Party and the Christian People's Party give them no help. They don't provide an alternative. With all due respect – that’s not how this case came about. Some crimes are committed online, for example: the exchange of images of abuses of children. It is not new that traffic data are used in the investigation of such and other crimes. It's happening today. The revolutionary new thing would be if the Storting made the network an “exploratory zone” and declared that no matter how serious the acts may be, the right to have its data erased should go ahead of society’s need to investigate and punish. It's a mockery of the term 'liberal' to define it as being willing to say to criminals: be so good, here's the button. If you want to delete the proof, click No to be researched and punished. In what other area of society would there at all be discussion about the possibility of equipping offenders with the ability to decide for themselves whether the tracks they leave behind could be used by the police or not? The idea appears to be completely monstrous, but it is in practice the resistance to storage obligations: society should refrain from obtaining information. Many people have made the impression that the state is now going to follow what we're doing. Among these are the Dagsnewens Hege Ulstein, who on December 1, with reference to WikiLeaks' publication of secret public documents, wrote: ‘If they can’t keep their own secrets, how are they going to keep ours?’ This overtramp fits into the array of misleading headlines in the media. The country is not the one to store traffic data. This ‘de’ that Ulstein fears does not exist. It is her own provider of network services that will store her traffic data. My supplier will store mine. Currently, data from electronic communications are stored in some cases without legal support, often for a short time and sometimes longer than permitted. The bill will clean up this. The proposal also implies that data currently stored for commercial reasons, billing, are to be stored below according to the needs of the Community, crime prevention. It makes it possible to investigate and prosecute serious crime. In addition, the requirements for storage itself are strengthened. Concession obligations are imposed on providers, personnel authorisation requirements are imposed, encryption regulations are in place and the obligation for suppliers to delete data that are no longer mandatory for storage is tightened up. The police will then have to walk the road to a court in order to obtain data. Normally, they must have concrete suspicions of serious crime – crime with a penalty framework of four years or more. In addition, they must demonstrate that the data will be essential for the investigation. It is incomprehensible that this strict regime of some is referred to as monitoring. It has nothing to do with monitoring – on the contrary. The proposal balances the fight against crime and the protection of privacy in a way that strengthens legal certainty for all. In addition, Norwegian police are given the same tools as their European counterparts. It's important. If the rule of law is to operate and open, it is important that privacy is safeguarded. At the same time, it is essential that serious crime is effectively combated.

The result is simple: those who plan to commit criminal acts take advantage of these possibilities and thus circumvent registration. The result is that those who are then potential offenders – that is, criminals – are not registered, but law-abiding citizens who really have nothing to hide and who have no intention of committing crime are registered. Then it becomes strange to me to introduce a data storage directive with the result that we do what makes a right-wing state worthy, that you are innocent until the opposite is proven. ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-04.ana.ud528e1796	I'll take the floor for Prop. 50 S for consent to the inclusion of the EU Data Storage Directive in the EEA Agreement. My colleagues from the Right have previously gone through the agreement concluded between the Right and the Labour Party on Prop. 49 L and the reason why the Right finds it possible to give such consent. I will not go further into the substance of the directive and the agreement, but will nevertheless allow me to point out what appears during this debate and the voting as a great paradox to us who are sceptical of the directive. The representative Per Sandberg said in the debate earlier today that ‘the absence of surveillance is well-being’. How, then, it is possible that the alternative of the Progress Party to implementing the Data Storage Directive is to increase, among others. the use of telephone interception? In the Prop settings. 49 L writes the party, among others the need for police intervention can be addressed without the Data Storage Directive and, in their view, ‘communication control, communication interception, screening and infiltration, instruments on which greater efforts should be made’. These are, of course, forms of monitoring against which the Right and other parties place strong constraints, precisely because there are very interventional instruments and because, unlike the Data Storage Directive, these instruments monitor the content of the communication. The Progress Party is in the position to amend the police law and lower the threshold in order to enable the police to operate so-called preventive surveillance for possible criminal acts. If you are concerned about privacy, this setting seems to be a bit funny. This case is one of several in the last years in which it has been discussed to use the so-called right of reservation in the EEA Agreement, or the right of veto, as it is also called. The right of reservation is governed by Article 102(1) of the EEA Agreement. 5, which states that the exercise of the right of reservation results in ‘the relevant part of the Annex being considered temporarily suspended’. What is the relevant part of the Annex may often be difficult to establish. In practice, decisions will be taken by the EU in a situation where no agreement on the relevant legislation is reached. Although the Norwegian side should have good legal cards on hand in certain cases, the EU decision will be just as political as legal. I'll get back to that a little bit. Of course, the fact that the right of reservation has never been used makes it difficult to make clear predictions about the consequences in both directions. However, I record that Professor Fredrik Sejersted in the committee's hearing on questions from my co-representative Trygve Schlagsvold Vedum replied that the right of reservation is real. But it's never meant to be free. Article 102 states that the agreement shall be so-called dynamic and uniform. There are many legal and political arguments that can be drawn in both directions as to the consequences that a possible right of reservation would have for the EEA Agreement: on the one hand, arguments that would indicate that it did not have such a significant impact, inter alia. that the Directive can be said to lie within the external borders of the EEA Agreement, that little of the rest of the Agreement is concerned, that the Directive has been in dispute within the EU and can thus provide an increased understanding of any Norwegian reservation law, and that Norway has been extremely loyal to the principle of dynamic uniformity for 17 years. On the other hand, you can use arguments against. There is widespread reluctance to let the EFTA States choose a little à la carte what they want to be included in directives. The reservation could also mean different market conditions. One argument that can actually be used both ways is that the fact that the directive is controversial in the EU may also be an argument against allowing a foreign country to avoid implementing it. I note that the government is particularly concerned about Norwegian participation in European telecommunications regulatory cooperation in a possible reservation against the Data Storage Directive. Let me also underline that reservation when the EEA Agreement was negotiated was considered a safety valve for matters where the central national interests were adversely affected. I think you can conclude that it is not unfair to use the right to reserve because it is part of the agreement, but it is not a loyalty account that Norway has worked up over many years. So evaluation of use must be an assessment of different interests. In view of this, the right agrees with the government’s proposal to consent to the inclusion of the Data Storage Directive in the EEA Agreement.

It's such a trend in our society already, and it's a trend that should worry us all. Technology gives us great opportunities. It gives us opportunities to make society more efficient in different ways. It gives us opportunities to communicate more and better, and it gives us opportunities to detect and avert crime. But new technologies also present difficult challenges. It requires us to discuss in principle the importance of borders in our society. There is a worrying tendency today to want to exchange freedom for security, or illusions about security. It is a shared responsibility for us to prevent such development. There has been a discussion of the extent to which the Data Storage Directive will actually improve the Norwegian police's ability to fight crime. We rely on such important issues of principle to dominate the debate on the European Union.
