ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-04.ana.ud528e1949	The EEA competition law is concerned, but deserves further mention in this debate, on the basis of some fundamental issues. Is the veto right? When should it be used? Does it have consequences for Norway to use the veto right — in which case? When Stortinget acceded to the EEA Agreement on 15 October 1992, Prime Minister Gro Harlem Brundtland was challenged by SV’s then major representative Erik Solheim. He claimed that the use of the EEA Agreement provision on veto would lead to serious problems with our partners. To this Harlem Brundtland replied: ‘And we will be prepared to use the right granted by the agreement, to oppose the proposal becoming a common EEA rule, if we consider it necessary.’ So far, this is the only time the Stortinget has discussed the veto right. It is therefore important to use speaking time on this topic in today’s debate. The enlargement of the EU and the reduction in the number of EFTA countries have increased the importance of the EEA Agreement for Norway. Pure trade balance shows this. Although the right of veto for Norway is real and implies political freedom from a legal point of view, the political space for the use of the right to veto has been reduced – that is my claim – since the EEA Agreement was signed. But of course, in a given case, we can use it. However, my claim is that any Norwegian use of the veto right has more adverse effects on Norway today than was the starting point for the conclusion of the EEA Agreement. Let me start by saying something about the formal process of new EU legislation after it has been adopted in the EU. When the Commission submits a proposal for new legislation to the Council and the European Parliament, Norway and the other EFTA/EEA countries are given the text of the proposal. We assess whether the proposals are EEA-relevant, and if so, the internal evaluation of the proposals may begin. The issue of the inclusion of EU legislation in the EEA Agreement is therefore presented to the EEA Committee as the conclusion of a thorough information and consultation process between the EFTA and EU Contracting Parties. The process is aimed at reaching an agreement. Fortunately, this almost always happens. If a country had constitutional problems with its content or claimed other fundamental national interests, a compromise was reached during the negotiations on the EEA Agreement. That will is still present. In fact, this is a fundamental prerequisite for cooperation. Norway is not alone in having a position. Our special assessments are not always as special as we think, but are often shared by EU Member States. Often we stand together in a Nordic block. Once compromises have been reached, Norwegian interests have been safeguarded in the process across national borders. The action space for the countries is established. Data storage is such an example where EU countries have discussed and concluded through a compromise. If there is no solution in the EEA Committee, the matter will be referred to the EEA Council, i.e. the Commission and the participating countries at ministerial level. If, despite long-term attempts to find a system in the framework of EEA cooperation, Norway decides to use the right of veto, the relevant part of the EEA Agreement to which that provision applies will be suspended, not only between Norway and the EU, but all EFTA countries and the EU. The unanimity principle in the EEA Committee implies this. If one EFTA country disagrees, EU legislation will not be implemented for them either. As a result, Norway, Iceland and Liechtenstein are no longer allowed to continue exporting to the EU on the basis of the old and often less stringent rules. In other words, simply: Norwegian producers who want to operate on the European market still need to adapt to the rules in force there in order to export to the EU. The possibility of veto being used in the EEA Agreement is important and is real. Nevertheless, my review today illustrates that there must be very special national considerations that Norway can rely on for the veto right in the EEA Agreement to be effectively applied. The implementation of the Data Storage Directive in Norwegian law is definitely not such a case.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-04.ana.ud528e2041	The issue of the implementation of the Data Storage Directive in Norwegian law has been demanding. The debate has largely focused on privacy issues, and there have been good arguments both for and against implementation. But it surprises me when someone says that those who are in favour of the directive are at the same time against privacy. I think that's not right. The proposal we are going to vote on today involves several ways of strengthening privacy. Even today, echo operators store data from electronic communications for short or longer periods of time for commercial purposes. Some people deliver data to the police when the police ask for it. Others require a court order before they deliver. Some providers delete data when they have to, but not everyone does. Better rules will hopefully help fix this. At the same time, there are some data, and I am thinking in particular about IP addresses, which according to the legislation today have to be deleted after a very short time. This is unfortunate for the investigation of a number of criminal offences in which this type of electronic trace is crucial to police work. That's why we're making sure to fix this right now. This means a little longer storage time for the data that the police may need in their work. At the same time, I understand that some people experience this as a privacy challenge. We have taken this challenge seriously in the proposal for new legislation. When we now impose storage obligations, we take a number of steps to ensure the privacy of the data subjects. First of all, and I think that's very central, we're getting clear rules on when the police are going to be able to get data from the echo operators. It shall no longer be sufficient to send an application for exemption from the obligation of professional secrecy to the postal and telecommunications authorities. Under the new rules, the police are required to obtain a court order before they can request information from the provider. I have confidence in the Post and Telecommunications Authority and that they have made a thorough assessment of these matters so far. At the same time, I believe that legal recognition is an even better guarantee that a thorough assessment is made of the police's need for data against the data subjects' privacy needs. I am sure that Norwegian courts will make these assessments in a good way and that they will ensure that privacy concerns are given the place they deserve in these assessments. Secondly, there will be tighter conditions for the police to be able to provide data. There must be a suspicion of serious criminal offences. So there are penalties that we're not in the vicinity of having today. In fact, no criminal penalties are required at all today for acts that are intended to underpin police access to data from electronic communications. At this point, I believe there is no doubt that the changes in the rules we are debating today are good privacy measures. They limit police access to data to situations where data are actually necessary for the police's important work. This criterion of necessity is essential in terms of privacy. The agreement between the Right and the Workers' Party has provided for further data protection measures. The supervisory work of the Data Protection Authority has revealed that not all providers have equal security in their systems. The agreement helps to clean it up and tighten the rules for securing the stored data. The introduction of the obligation to store and process data makes the Data Protection Authority aware of all those who are required to store echo data in accordance with the new rules. The concessions may lay down specific conditions for the protection of privacy. In addition, I assume that the Data Protection Authority strengthens its oversight of the ecosystem sector in order to ensure compliance with the conditions set. The data surveillance authority shall be empowered to impose encryption where it is considered necessary to ensure the data. In addition, all access to the data shall be logged. Any reading, disclosure or other processing of the data will leave traces in the system. This should include, inter alia: prevent and, where appropriate, detect unauthorised access. In addition, we are in fact imposing not only a storage obligation, but also a clear obligation to delete the data once the mandatory storage period has expired, i.e. after six months. This is a clear indication that providers have an obligation not only to store data for crime control purposes but also to delete data for privacy reasons. Overall, I believe that the bill, as it now stands, goes a little further than today's actual storage practices of most providers. At the same time, the legislative proposal and the amendments of the majority will significantly strengthen privacy. Judicial control and clear penalties provide significant sanctions. Many security measures to prevent unauthorised access to data and ensure that data is only provided when they are actually necessary for police work reinforce this. An important aspect of the debate on the implementation of the Data Storage Directive, and the high interest in the population on the issue, have contributed to raising the interest and awareness of privacy. That's very good! I'm pretty sure there's not a lot of echo operators in the future who could say that they don't know the rules on the storage and deletion of traffic and location data. Because now the rules are getting clearer, and we're going to make sure that they get well-known and that they're being observed. It will also be a strength for privacy.

And when one knows that one can use the same phone and call through Skype or talk through Facebook or use Hotmail or Gmail, or use foreign SIM cards on a cash card in a phone, it says to itself that if it is so important, and if it is to be taken so many, they are very stupid those who cannot avoid it. That's why I think it's hard to reverse the principle that you're innocent until the opposite is proven, and monitor the whole community just because one believes that this should first of all be so important, and second that one should take so many. I don't think it's worth it, even if I see any arguments. That's why I have to say that I don't think a person can say that one argument is good enough – and the next. There are always some arguments that are good enough, but if you continue like this, one will move into a society that one does not want in the future. That is the decision that we are making today.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-04.ana.ud528e2203	All providers of telecommunications and the Internet are thus required to store all traffic data in view of the subsequent criminal prosecution of customers. This departs from two principles: first, the basic rule that personal data should be used only for the purpose for which they were collected. What has so far been banned for private purposes is now becoming a norm for the state. Second, the principle of investigation: that no preparation for criminal prosecution is to be done without concrete suspicion. The representative of Kolberg said in a previous post that data collection was only for the perpetrators of serious crime. I'm afraid that's not true. The case concerns the collection and storage of data for all users. If we assumed that no information would go astray and be misused by the criminals you say you would fight, if we assumed that no one in the administration and police could do wrong or wrong, if we assumed that a democratically run state could really ensure that no one would break its own security rules, even then we would still face an instrument that has a cost side greater than money. Society where law enforcement is the result of external control and detection risks is fundamentally different from countries where law enforcement is based on incorporated standards and citizens have made their own responsible choices. Of course, we all agree not to use torture or degrading treatment to impose confessions, without any reason being accused of not wanting to fight crime. But there is a long civilisational development behind such a position. On the other hand, it appears that policy in democratic countries has not yet been able to set any fundamental limits on the use of technological instruments, only degree limits. The limits have so far been set by courts which have assessed the interventions against constitutional protection of individuals. The Directive is part of a change of mood triggered by events for approx. 10 years ago. The size was dealt with five years ago by extended and in-depth investigation methods. Even then, it was said that a perfect balance was found between privacy and police methods, not least because the investigation principle, which is now abandoned, was retained. Unfortunately, the directive has the potential for further slippage. Already in view of what is serious crime, there has been a slip while the matter has been on the agenda. During the debate we have heard that data storage is almost a higher expression of privacy. It is based on the misconception that the individual's interests are fully integrated in and cared for by the state. The greatest mistakes in human history have been made on the basis of such misconceptions. The directive is no profit for the development of justice, and I would also like to take the consequences of the subsequent vote.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-04.ana.ud528e2224	I think we should keep the Data Protection Tab high when we discuss investigation methods for the police, because it must never be the police that dictate the elected authorities on the subject of anti-crime tools. The tools must come after political balances. But privacy is also about the basic security of each individual because society has a responsibility to prevent crime. It is also important to ensure that if we are exposed to crime, our chances of being exposed to crime are high. We don't necessarily need more monitoring, but the goal must be to have a smarter use of traffic data that we can access. The legislative proposal for the implementation of the EU Data Storage Directive means that more stringent requirements will be imposed than today on the use of traffic data in criminal investigations. The Data Storage Directive obliges the providers of communications such as the Internet and telephony to store traffic data, to which they are not obliged today. This means that today there is a great deal of uncertainty in each case where the police consider that there is reason to retrieve data from individual companies, when companies have different practices, and also when the handling and securing of data is too poor. The agreement between the Right and Workers' Party goes a long way in tightening up the guidelines for the use of traffic data, which I believe is a double victory for privacy, as the data collected is made more secure forewarned and more difficult for uninvited eyes, while giving the police a better opportunity to resolve more cases. The Data Storage Directive is important for the resolution of serious crime, such as child abuse on networks, terror and organised crime that goes through several countries. Out of 1,450 criminal cases in which criminal penalties had been imposed for murder, serious drug crimes, serious crime and sexual assault, traffic data had been collected in 52pst. In 82 p.m. of cases where traffic data had been collected, the main investigator was of the opinion that traffic data had a significant impact on the outcome of the case. Traffic data must now be stored for six months and the maximum volume of traffic data is requested here. But I'm pleased that the length of the storage period is some of the things that are to be evaluated after four years, because in some of the major and major criminal cases, traffic data is also needed in the period six months to 12 months. First, any disclosure of data shall be subject to judicial review and, second, data shall only be provided to the police where there is ‘disputeable grounds for suspecting’ a criminal offence which, under the general rule, has a penalty framework of more than four years. Thirdly, it is combined with a requirement that the provision of data must be essential for the investigation. Finally, these three last points show that the Directive will have very little impact on our everyday lives.
ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-04.ana.ud528e2247	For a long time we have taken freedom and our rights for granted. Principles such as freedom of expression and human dignity have been carriers of our democracy, and the idea that someone would rock these principles has never been present before. As mentioned earlier in the debate, this directive is an annihilation of one of the most important principles on which our country of law is founded, namely the principle that you are innocent until the opposite is proven. In this principle lies, among other things: not to be investigated unless one is suspected of planning, contributing to or having committed a criminal act. This is strongly reflected in both our legal position and in the European Convention on Human Rights. The introduction of the Data Storage Directive will impose a collection of data on all, without taking into account whether they are suspected or not. This makes us move towards a nation that is then based on distrust over trust. Yes, it is true that information about a lot is already stored. Credit card usage, telephone usage, bombings and the like are often stored in order to be able to bill users afterwards. The important change is that the directive now requires all telecommunications companies to save who you call, where you call from, when you call, how long you talk, who you send SMS to, etc. So, everyone has to be investigated as a criminal without any suspicion or charge of participation in criminal acts. Now that the state of Norway is monitoring its citizens, I think it is frightening. It gives an unpleasant feeling that big brother sees me even more than before. I agree with the Council of State Lighthouse that the criminals will probably be in front of the technology, that they will be able to figure out ways to get away, while we are the others who are not criminals, who will feel uncomfortable by being monitored. We are going into a future that really rockes our freedom of expression and the human dignity.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-04.ana.ud528e2268	The purpose of the Data Storage Directive is to detect, investigate and prosecute serious crime. Today's practice means that data traffic is stored from 10 to 5 months. The right will, through voting in the Storting today, increase it to six months. IP addresses are stored today for three weeks. The no lots and data protection will further increase the storage of IP addresses. It is supported by the right, but we want to limit it to the minimum of six months in the directive. In the old days, the police could wait for the criminals at a hidden meeting place. Today's technology makes meeting places change. ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-04.ana.ud528e2813	In Denmark, 82 000 registrations were registered in 2010 as a result of the requirements of the Data Storage Directive – per person. It is difficult for many to see the impact of the Directive. One can imagine that there was an inspector next to you and noted who you called, when you called, how long the call lasted, and where the receiver was when you called him – or that there was an inspector at the mailbox and noted who you sent letters to, and where you were when the letter was sent. The question is: is it just the fact that the technology for monitoring this electronically does not yet exist, which means that the Right Party and the Workers' Party do not vote for the monitoring also of letter mail? In principle, that is exactly what will happen with the Data Storage Directive. The difference between this physical monitoring and the Data Storage Directive is that the latter happens without the attention of the population because it is done electronically. By following the principles of the Data Storage Directive, it might be said that it would also be necessary to register who you are visiting, where they live, how long you are there, if you delivered something from you, and if you got something back. For the time being, there are no feasible electronic methods for recording this. But the question is nevertheless obvious: is there a fundamental difference between this and what the Storting imposes today on the Norwegian people? Is it just because it is so difficult to physically monitor who is visiting who, that this is not part of the Data Storage Directive, that the electronic solution is temporarily missing? And: If it were technically feasible, if the technology existed, would the Workers' Party and the Right have agreed to electronic control of all visits to other people? Some may visit others to plan a criminal act. What arguments would the Workers' Party and the Right possibly have used against that type of monitoring, where there is in reality no fundamental difference between the monitoring of physical visits and that contained in the Data Storage Directive? The future will show which extensions the directive will take on gradually, and which links will be made with other registers – from banks, hubs, public registers and foreign registers – all in the proper name to help the police, but across the security and privacy of ordinary people. The Data Storage Directive crosses a crucial and security threshold, and the only right thing to do today, when it is likely to be adopted, is to postpone consent to the Directive until the EU revision is available.

It is precisely when coercive measures are applied to individuals that the presumption of innocence should be kept high. When people are held in custody, the presumption of innocence applies. You are not considered guilty until you have been convicted in a court of law, and the judgment is enforceable. It's the presumption of innocence. The fact that the police have access to see who you have called, when, and possibly where, you were, does not violate the presumption of innocence. It is therefore important for me to say that in both space listening, which the majority in this Chamber have been involved in adopting, custody prison, which everyone has been involved in adopting, and the seizure of property is fully related to the presumption of innocence, and one can be fully freed. Yes, in many cases it is precisely when the presumption of innocence is to be applied, and there is a need to be treated as innocent until criminal conviction is possible. Finally, I would like to comment – it was Special Representative Ramsøy who mentioned it – that there are no ‘limits’ for what the police will demand, and that there are no limits to what some would accept, if only the police demand it. I believe that this is a fundamental mistrust of both the Police Directorate, Kripos, the Police Security Service, the National Attorney – yes, for that matter also the Police Joint Union. I have much more confidence in these agencies and organizations. I believe that the national lawyer when we adopted space snooping in the previous period, it was just a moderate element and contributed to the fact that it was not as extensive as proposed by the then government. It may have done so in the Central Party, but in the Right the debate has not concerned the three magical letters DLD, but it has focused on 1) the important principles on whether to store on the basis of commercial interests or social interests, and 2) on the concrete substance of changes in a national law. The majority in the Right Group ended up saying that on the basis of a fundamental discussion, it was right to create a law that was the basis for storing information based on democratic decisions in the Storting, rather than that the information should be based only on what should be of commercial interest to the company at any time. When the Right Group was finished with the principle discussion, on which one has spent most time, a discussion started about what one then thought it was actually necessary to store, based on the purpose of storage, and then what important privacy measures had to be put in place to counteract what everyone agrees on is a challenge – at least everyone in the Right believes is a challenge – namely that when you start storing more information, it is in itself a privacy challenge. A number of demands were then made by one party in the negotiations, both with the five parties – the so-called privacy alliance – and with the Workers' Party. Those requirements have been passed. This means that what the Storting adopts today is a national solution where a storehold based on what the Storting believes is necessary to store, and not based on what is commercially interesting to store at any time, and where a come up with a series of measures to counter what privacy is a challenge, which is always present by storing more information. That's why the Right Group supports what the majority here supports.

ParlaMint-NO_2011-04-04.ana.ud528e3306	I notice that, President! The matter we are now dealing with, Innst. 262 S for 2010–2011 Changes in the State budget for 2011 under the Ministry of Justice and Police, Prop. 72 S for 2010–2011 is initially a fairly simple matter. It is a matter of paying part-payment of deliveries for the first stage of construction of Nødnett, for which the supplier should have actually been delivered and paid for in the financial year 2010, but which is due to delays in delivery, which we are all familiar with, now is due to be paid early in 2011. So this bill has to be paid before the revision. Therefore, a separate proposition is promoted. This was notified at the effusion last year, so there is no news either. I put forward a unanimous opinion on the matter and would like to point out that the Committee is satisfied that this change in the State budget represents only a time shift of a payment milestone from the 2010 budget, see. Prop. 21 S for 2010–2011, without representing a cost increase in the project. Here I could have stopped my post, but I find reason to comment on some comments from the opposition. First, to the comment on the expectation that the evaluation of the first phase will be quickly presented to the Stortinget, so that the Stortinget can quickly take a decision to possibly move forward with the development of the new emergency network across the country. I refer – as has also been done in previous cases on Emergency Network – to the Ministry of Justice and Police's Prop. 1 S, which sets out the two-step decision of the Storting in connection with the treatment of the Innst. S No 104 for 2006-2007, cf. St. pp. No 30 for 2006-2007. It also shows that the government will come back to Stortinget with a detailed assessment of the issue of nationwide development, i.e. step 2 of the Emergency Network, when evaluation and quality assurance have been completed. Furthermore, allocation needs have been taken into account in 2011 as a result of any decision on nationwide development. So to the majority’s observation that several municipalities report increased costs related to the operation of the new Emergency Network, and that the real costs of the municipality must be made visible and fully compensated. To that end, the development of a new radiocommunication system for fire services, police and health services, i.e. emergency networks, is decided by the Stortinget. All emergency budgets need to update existing emergency contact solutions, and Emergency networks are a common system to meet the needs of all departments. All municipalities, based on fire and health legislation, are obliged to establish and operate a proper emergency service for the municipality fire service and for the part of the community health service. The development of the emergency network as decided and implemented means that the State finances the entire investment in new radio networks, communication centres and radio terminals for emergency situations, including for municipal users. The total investment costs, if the Storting adopts nationwide expansion, are above 4 billion. kr. For municipalities, the option would be in a short time and with its own means to renew and build separate emergency networks that meet today’s requirements. Although the State covers all the investment costs, municipalities, as at present, must continue to bear the costs of their use of the emergency service in the fire service and in the municipal health service. Emergency user costs correspond to the total operating costs. These are distributed among users according to a proposed model. Both KS and municipal representatives have participated in the development of the user payment model. It has been suggested that the model be evaluated when it has been in use for some time, and when you have more experience. It's in St.prp. No 30 for 2006-2007 provided that, overall, municipalities will not have a significant increase in their costs of emergency contact. Several studies have been carried out on the cost of emergency contact before the introduction of the Emergency Network, most recently by SINTEF Health in the autumn of 2008. SINTEF Health has similarly estimated the costs of the facilities after the deployment of Emergency Networks based on the experience of Step 1. This information will be included in the evaluation base, and the user costs will be mentioned in the forthcoming call for proposals on Emergency Networks notified in spring 2011. So to the majority's remarks about the delay. The contract concluded for the supply of Emergency Network provides the supplier with total responsibility. The supplier shall provide a complete, ready-to-use installation where all technical components are to be completed and interact before the State acquires ownership. The contract also includes an operational and maintenance agreement that gives the supplier responsibility for building up and providing the ongoing operator services for the entire life of the emergency network. The supplier is responsible for planning and implementing the deployment of the radio network. Furthermore, the supplier is responsible for delivering equipment to fire, police and health communication centres. Given that the supplier complies with the present progress plan, this implies a delay of about three years for the last parts of the delivery. This is the committee informed about the past, and I just remind you that this digital emergency network is among the most advanced in its class in the world, and the many stakeholders are making the project Norway's perhaps most complex ICT project ever. The uncertainty associated with the gap between the two lines of development has created challenges for all involved, and it is now important to go further, too, in order to avoid the loss of important competences and costly costs. The Committee is aware that the government has announced, as I have mentioned, a big message on further nationwide expansion in the spring of 2011, and the Stortinget will then have a broad and profound debate on the matter. That matter is about today, in short, we are only going to decide to grant back the funds that were adjusted in the fall of 2010, so that the state can meet the payment milestones that have been shifted to the spring of 2011. The matter is important enough, because it is about completing step 1 and getting on with the process of expanding this network that will benefit the whole country.
